 Proposition 6.5 . 
 
 For fixed  k k  and  n ≥ 3 ​ k − 1 n\geq 3k-1 , 
 

 
 
 S n ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k ( 1 ) = 2 ⋅ 3 k − 1 ( k − 1 ) ! ​ n k − 1 + O ⁡ ( n k − 2 ) and S n ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k ( 2 ) = 2 ⋅ 3 k − 2 ( k − 2 ) ! ​ n k − 2 + O ⁡ ( n k − 3 ) ​  as  ​ n → ∞ . S^{(1)}_{n;\,\cdot,n-2k}=\frac{2\cdot 3^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}n^{k-1}+O(n^{k-2})\hskip 10.0pt\text{and}\hskip 10.0ptS^{(2)}_{n;\,\cdot,n-2k}=\frac{2\cdot 3^{k-2}}{(k-2)!}n^{k-2}+O(n^{k-3})\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Take  α ∈ E n ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k \alpha\in E_{n;\,\cdot,n-2k} , and let
 𝒜 \mathscr{A}  be the union of the convex components of  α \alpha  to the left of and including the leftmost transversal ( lilt );  𝒜 \mathscr{A}  determines the contribution  σ ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \sigma(\mathscr{A})  of  α \alpha  to  S n ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k ( i ) S_{n;\,\cdot,n-2k}^{(i)} , independently of  n n . Now consider the set of all elements with a fixed configuration  𝒜 \mathscr{A} , which we may think of as a  macrostate . We shall partition this set into  microstates  as next described.
Let the convex components of  α \alpha  to the right of  𝒜 \mathscr{A}  be partitioned into isomorphism classes (some empty)  A ι A_{\iota} , for  ι ∈ I \iota\in I , an index set excluding  0 0  and containing  1 , 2 , 3 , 3 ′ 1,2,3,3^{\prime}  and other unspecified symbols. We shall specify that  A 1 A_{1}  contains precisely all  0 0 -paths (that is, all singleton components), that all  2 2 -cycles on adjacent vertices make up  A 2 A_{2} , and that there are two classes of convex components using three vertices,  A 3 A_{3}  and  A 3 ′ A_{3^{\prime}} , which contain

respectively. We write  a ι = | A ι | a_{\iota}=|A_{\iota}|  and say that two idempotents belong to the same microstate if they share the  lilt   𝒜 \mathscr{A}  and their sequences  ( a ι ) (a_{\iota})  are equal. Naturally the  a ι a_{\iota}  satisfy constraints. Let the number of vertices in  𝒜 \mathscr{A}  be  L L , and let  v ι v_{\iota}  represent the number of vertices in any element (a convex component) of  A ι A_{\iota} . Then 
 

 
 
 ∑ v ι ​ a ι = n − L = ∑ a ι + ∑ ι ≠ 1 ( v ι − 1 ) ​ a ι . \sum v_{\iota}a_{\iota}=n-L=\sum a_{\iota}+\sum_{\iota\neq 1}(v_{\iota}-1)a_{\iota}. 
 
 (10) 
 
 Let  J J  be the subset of  I I  indexing all the classes with components having at least one path (so  1 , 3 , 3 ′ , ⋯ ∈ J 1,3,3^{\prime},\dots\in J ). Since there are  n − 2 ​ k n-2k  paths, and  𝒜 \mathscr{A}  contains at least one, 
 

 
 
 ∑ ι ∈ J a ι ≤ n − 2 ​ k − 1 . \sum_{\iota\in J}a_{\iota}\leq n-2k-1. 
 
 
 
 
 The point of all this is that the number of idempotents in a microstate  ( a ι ) (a_{\iota})  is given by the multinomial coefficient 
 

 
 
 ( a 1 + a 2 + ⋯ ) ! a 1 ! a 2 ! ⋯ = { n − L − ∑ ι ≠ 1 ( v ι − 1 ) ​ a ι } [ a 2 + a 3 + a 3 ′ + ⋯ ] a 2 ! a 3 ! ⋯ , \frac{(a_{1}+a_{2}+\cdots)!}{a_{1}!a_{2}!\cdots}=\frac{\{n-L-\sum_{\iota\neq 1}(v_{\iota}-1)a_{\iota}\}^{[a_{2}+a_{3}+a_{3^{\prime}}+\cdots]}}{a_{2}!a_{3}!\cdots}, 
 
 
 where equation ( 10 ) is used, and  x [ y ] x^{[y]}  means the descending factorial of  y y  factors. Thus to find the term in  S ( i ) S^{(i)}  of highest degree in  n n , we encounter the integer programming problem 
 

 
 
 maximise  ​ z \displaystyle\text{maximise }z

 ≤ n − 2 ​ k − 1 . \displaystyle\leq n-2k-1. 
 
 
 
 
 Now  v ι = ι v_{\iota}=\iota  for  ι ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 } \iota\in\{1,2,3\}  and  v 3 ′ = 3 v_{3^{\prime}}=3 . A feasible solution in which there is some convex component in an  A ι A_{\iota}  with  v ι ≥ 4 v_{\iota}\geq 4  may be replaced by two or more convex components using more paths or the same number of paths and some 2-cycles, improving the objective while preserving feasibility; the same is true of the  lt . Thus solutions have  a ι = 0 a_{\iota}=0  for  ι ∉ { 1 , 2 , 3 , 3 ′ } \iota\notin\{1,2,3,3^{\prime}\} , every path (including the  lt ) is either a 0-path or a 2-path, and the second constraint holds with equality. Moreover,  a 3 + a 3 ′ a_{3}+a_{3^{\prime}}  may be treated as a single variable.

t\end{array}\right), 
 
 (11) 
 
 for  0 ≤ t ≤ k − ℓ 0\leq t\leq k-\ell .
The objective value is  k − ℓ k-\ell , so we should choose  ℓ \ell  minimal subject to  σ ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) ≠ 0 \sigma(\mathscr{A})\neq 0 , which is  ℓ = 1 \ell=1  for  S ( 1 ) S^{(1)}  and  ℓ = 2 \ell=2  for  S ( 2 ) S^{(2)} .
Thus the microstates contributing the highest order terms to  S ( i ) S^{(i)}  are identified. Moreover,  σ ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) = 2 \sigma(\mathscr{A})=2  in both cases. It remains to count the idempotents (interface graphs) that have  ℓ \ell  2-cycle convex components on the vertex set  { 1 , … , 2 ​ ℓ } \{1,\dots,2\ell\} , a  lt  on vertex  { 2 ​ ℓ + 1 } \{2\ell+1\} , and satisfy ( 11 ). This number is the sum of multinomial coefficients 
 

 
 
 ∑ t = 0 k − ℓ ∑ a 3 + a 3 ′ = t ( n − k − ℓ − t − 1 ) ! ( n − 2 ​ k − t − 1 ) ! ​ a 2 ! ​ a 3 ! ​ a 3 ′ ! = ∑ t = 0 k − ℓ ∑ a 3 + a 3 ′ = t ( n − k − ℓ − t − 1 ) [ k − ℓ ] ( k − ℓ − t ) ! ​ a 3 ! ​ a 3 ′ ! , \sum_{t=0}^{k-\ell}\sum_{a_{3}+a_{3^{\prime}}=t}\frac{(n-k-\ell-t-1)!}{(n-2k-t-1)!\;a_{2}!\;a_{3}!\;a_{3^{\prime}}!}=\sum_{t=0}^{k-\ell}\sum_{a_{3}+a_{3^{\prime}}=t}\frac{(n-k-\ell-t-1)^{[k-\ell]}}{(k-\ell-t)!\;a_{3}!\;a_{3^{\prime}}!}, 
 
 
 and we need to select those that contribute to the leading term. The coefficient of the term in  n k − ℓ n^{k-\ell}  is  ∑ t = 0 k − ℓ ∑ a 3 + a 3 ′ = t 1 ( k − ℓ − t ) ! ​ a 3 ! ​ a 3 ′ ! \sum_{t=0}^{k-\ell}\sum_{a_{3}+a_{3^{\prime}}=t}\frac{1}{(k-\ell-t)!\;a_{3}!\;a_{3^{\prime}}!} . To evaluate this, note that 
 

 
 
 ( k − ℓ ) ! ( k − ℓ − t ) ! ​ a 3 ! ​ a 3 ′ ! \frac{(k-\ell)!}{(k-\ell-t)!\;a_{3}!\;a_{3^{\prime}}!} 
 
 
 is the coefficient of  x k − ℓ − t ​ ( y + z ) t x^{k-\ell-t}(y+z)^{t}  in the expansion of  { x + ( y + z ) } k − ℓ \{x+(y+z)\}^{k-\ell} , and so 
 

 
 
 ∑ t = 0 k − ℓ ∑ a 3 + a 3 ′ = t 1 ( k − ℓ − t ) ! ​ a 3 ! ​ a 3 ′ ! = 3 k − ℓ ( k − ℓ ) ! . \sum_{t=0}^{k-\ell}\sum_{a_{3}+a_{3^{\prime}}=t}\frac{1}{(k-\ell-t)!\;\;a_{3}!\;a_{3^{\prime}}!}=\frac{3^{k-\ell}}{(k-\ell)!}. 
 
 
 The assertions of the rubric follow on multiplication by  σ ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) = 2 \sigma(\mathscr{A})=2 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 It follows that  ∇ k − 1 S n ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k ( 1 ) = 2 ⋅ 3 k − 1 \nabla^{k-1}S^{(1)}_{n;\,\cdot,n-2k}=2\cdot 3^{k-1}  and  ∇ k − 1 S n ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k ( 2 ) = 0 \nabla^{k-1}S^{(2)}_{n;\,\cdot,n-2k}=0  for  n ≥ 3 ​ k − 1 n\geq 3k-1 . This gives a result about counts of idempotents of depth  k k  which is evident in the data of Table 3. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.6 . 
 
 For fixed  k k  and  n ≥ 3 ​ k − 1 n\geq 3k-1 ,  ∇ k e n ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k = 3 k . \nabla^{k}e_{n;\,\cdot,n-2k}=3^{k}. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  z n ( k ) = e n ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k z^{(k)}_{n}=e_{n;\,\cdot,n-2k} , and assume for the purpose of induction that  ∇ k − 1 z n ( k − 1 ) = 3 k − 1 \nabla^{k-1}z^{(k-1)}_{n}=3^{k-1} , as indeed holds for  k = 2 k=2  by Proposition  6.3 . Then by Proposition  6.1  we have 
 

 
 
 e n ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k \displaystyle e_{n;\,\cdot,n-2k} 
 = \displaystyle= 
 e n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 1 − 2 ​ ( k − 1 ) + e n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k − 1 + S n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k − 1 ( 1 ) + S n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 2 ​ k − 1 ( 2 ) , \displaystyle e_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-1-2(k-1)}+e_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-2k-1}+S^{(1)}_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-2k-1}+S^{(2)}_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-2k-1}, 
 
 
 
 that is,  ​ z n ( k ) \displaystyle\text{that is, }z^{(k)}_{n} 
 = \displaystyle= 
 z n − 1 ( k − 1 ) + z n − 1 ( k ) + S n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 1 − 2 ​ k ( 1 ) + S n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 1 − 2 ​ k ( 2 ) , \displaystyle z^{(k-1)}_{n-1}+z^{(k)}_{n-1}+S^{(1)}_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-1-2k}+S^{(2)}_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-1-2k}, 
 
 
 and so 
 

 
 
 ∇ z n ( k ) = z n − 1 ( k − 1 ) + S n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 1 − 2 ​ k ( 1 ) + S n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 1 − 2 ​ k ( 2 ) . \nabla z^{(k)}_{n}=z^{(k-1)}_{n-1}+S^{(1)}_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-1-2k}+S^{(2)}_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-1-2k}. 
 
 
 Now applying the inductive hypothesis and using Proposition  6.5 , 
 

 
 
 ∇ k z n ( k ) \displaystyle\nabla^{k}z^{(k)}_{n} 
 = \displaystyle= 
 ∇ k − 1 z n − 1 ( k − 1 ) + ∇ k − 1 { S n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 1 − 2 ​ k ( 1 ) + S n − 1 ; ⋅ , n − 1 − 2 ​ k ( 2 ) } \displaystyle\nabla^{k-1}z^{(k-1)}_{n-1}+\nabla^{k-1}\{S^{(1)}_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-1-2k}+S^{(2)}_{n-1;\,\cdot,n-1-2k}\} 
 
 
 
 
 = \displaystyle= 
 3 k − 1 + 2 ⋅ 3 k − 1 + 0 \displaystyle 3^{k-1}+2\cdot 3^{k-1}+0 
 
 
 
 
 = \displaystyle= 
 3 k , \displaystyle 3^{k}, 
 
 
 as claimed.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We remark that there also seem to be similar patterns in the Motzkin table (Table  2 ). 
 
 
 Finally, we observe connections between the return statistics and the Kauffman idempotent numbers, reminiscent of the meandric numbers: from Proposition  4.4  (iii), we have  | E ⁡ ( 𝒥 n ξ ) | = e n ; 0 , ⋅ := ∑ p e n ; 0 , p |E(\mathscr{J}_{\!n}^{\xi})|=e_{n;0,\cdot}:=\sum_{p}e_{n;0,p} . Then from Proposition  6.1  we have 
 

 
 
 e n ; 0 , ⋅ \displaystyle e_{n;0,\cdot} 
 = \displaystyle= 
 0 + e n − 1 ; 0 , ⋅ + S n − 1 ; 1 , ⋅ ( 1 ) + S n − 1 ; 2 , ⋅ ( 2 ) , \displaystyle 0+e_{n-1;0,\cdot}+S^{(1)}_{n-1;1,\cdot}+S^{(2)}_{n-1;2,\cdot}\;, 
 
 
 
 giving ∇ e n ; 0 , ⋅ \displaystyle\text{giving}\hskip 30.00005pt\nabla e_{n;0,\cdot} 
 = \displaystyle= 
 S n − 1 ; 1 , ⋅ ( 1 ) + S n − 1 ; 2 , ⋅ ( 2 ) . \displaystyle S^{(1)}_{n-1;1,\cdot}+S^{(2)}_{n-1;2,\cdot}\;. 
 
 
 
 
 Further progress on these topics seems to require a better combinatorial understanding of the statistics  S ( 1 ) S^{(1)}  and  S ( 2 ) S^{(2)} . That there is indeed more to be discovered about the series of fascinating semigroups  𝒥 n \mathscr{J}_{\!n}  is strongly indicated by Figures  4  and  5 , which present eggbox diagrams  [ 10 ]  in which the  ℋ \mathscr{H} -classes are shaded if they contain an idempotent. The interested reader may consult Larsson’s thesis  [ 15 ]  or the article of Lau and FitzGerald  [ 16 ]  for more on the  𝒟 \mathscr{D} -structure of  𝒥 n \mathscr{J}_{\!n} . The bitmaps here were created by Dr A. Egri-Nagy from  𝒟 \mathscr{D} -class tables generated using the Semigroups package for GAP  [ 19 ] ; we thank him for sharing them. The reader who wishes to see more is referred to East  et alii   [ 5 ] .

 Figure 5:  Three  𝒟 \mathscr{D} -classes from the Jones monoid  𝒥 15 \mathscr{J}\!_{15} .

 Appendix A  Appendix: Algorithms 

 
 The formulæ  in Corollary  3.6  and equation ( 6 ) are amenable to parallel computation. Here we sketch an algorithm for the computation of the idempotent numbers by the method of Section 3. There is wide scope for choice of methods to represent and sequentially generate Dyck and Motzkin semi-words and perhaps also to identify components and count returns—inspiration may be found in Deutsch’s article  [ 3 ]  and its bibliography—so we do not trouble with those details here. 
 
 
 Outline of a general algorithm. 
Input  n n , the number of vertices. Partition the  ℛ \mathscr{R} -classes of  D D , given by Motzkin or Dyck semi-words or words of length  n n , into  k k  parts, say  R 1 ​ … ​ R k R_{1}\dots R_{k} . A ‘secretary’ or master processor  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0}  passes  R j R_{j}  (or sufficient information to sequentially generate it) to Processor  𝒫 j \mathcal{P}_{j} . 
 
 •  
 
 For each  υ ∈ R k \upsilon\in R_{k} ,  𝒫 j \mathcal{P}_{j}  runs through all the  ℒ \mathscr{L} -classes of  D D , indexed by Motzkin or Dyck  n n -words  λ \lambda  of matching rank 0 or 1, calculating as follows. 
 
 –  
 
 Concatenate to form the pair  ( υ , λ ) (\upsilon,\lambda) , representing  δ \delta  as in representation(iv) of Section  2.1 , checking for idempotency if  rank ⁡ ( δ ) = 1 \operatorname{rank}(\delta)=1 . Identify the levels, edges, returns and components of  δ \delta  as in Table  1 . 
 
 *  
 
 For each cycle component  θ \theta , count the returns and calculate  x θ = u θ ​ l θ x_{\theta}=u_{\theta}l_{\theta} , and then update  P = ∏ ( 1 + x θ ) P=\prod(1+x_{\theta})  and  K = ∏ x θ K=\prod x_{\theta} . (Note that if  θ \theta  has no returns on at least one side,  K = 0 K=0  and  P P  is unchanged.) It could also store 
 

 
 
 S 1 = ∑ x θ , S 2 = ∑ θ 1 < θ 2 x θ 1 x θ 2 , etc . S_{1}=\sum x_{\theta},\hskip 8.50012ptS_{2}=\sum_{\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}}x_{\theta_{1}}x_{\theta_{2}},\hskip 4.0pt\rm{etc.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 When all (necessary) components are done (dependent on parity),  𝒫 j \mathcal{P}_{j}  adds  P , K P,K  and the  S i S_{i}  to accumulators and goes to the next concatenation  δ ′ = υ ​ λ ′ \delta^{\prime}=\upsilon\lambda^{\prime}  ( ′ = {}^{\prime}=  successor) and repeats. 
 
 
 
 When the  ℒ \mathscr{L} -classes are all finished,  𝒫 j \mathcal{P}_{j}  increments the prefix  υ \upsilon  and repeats. When it has done all members of its domain  R j R_{j} , it returns the accumulated values  P , K , P,K,  and  S i S_{i}  to the secretary. 
 
 
 When all parts of  D D  are finished, the secretary processor reports the results  e n e_{n} , etc. 
 
 
 Code for methods used in comparison of times in Tables  7  and  8 .
Method 1 is a ‘brute force’ application of GAP, and ran out of memory at  n = 10 n=10  : 
 
 B := [ ]; 
 n := 1; Size( Idempotents( MotzkinMonoid( n ) ) );; t := time;; Add( B, t );; 
 n := n+1; Size( Idempotents( MotzkinMonoid( n ) ) );; t := time;; Add( B, t );; # execute this line as often as desired 
 B;
 
 
 Method 2 (dubbed ‘memoryless’) checks element-wise and accumulates: 
 
 MotzCount := function( n ) 
 local count, Mn, s;
 count := 0;
 Mn := MotzkinMonoid( n ); 
 for s in Iterator( Mn ) do 
 if s ˆ 2 = s then 
 count := count + 1; 
 fi; 
 od; 
 return count; 
 end; 
 A := [ ]; 
 n := 1;; MotzCount(n);; t := time;; Add( A, t );; 
 n := n+1; MotzCount(n);; t := time;; Add( A, t );; # again, repeat this 
 A;
